### **One-sentence summary**

Long-term acclimation to fluctuating light induces global reprogramming of gene expression in Arabidopsis, with strong interactions with time of day and leaf development stage.

### **Abstract**

 Natural-light environments are highly variable. Flexible adjustment between light energy utilization and photoprotection is therefore of vital importance for plant performance and fitness in the field. Short-term reactions to changing light intensity are triggered inside chloroplasts and leaves within seconds to minutes, whereas long-term adjustments proceed over hours and days, integrating multiple signals. While the mechanisms of long-term acclimation to light intensity have been studied by changing constant growth light intensity during the day, responses to fluctuating growth light intensity have rarely been inspected in detail. We performed transcriptome profiling in Arabidopsis (*Arabidopsis thaliana*) leaves to investigate long-term gene expression responses to fluctuating light (FL). In particular, we examined whether responses differ between young and mature leaves or between morning and at the end of the day. Our results highlight global reprogramming of gene expression under FL, including that of genes related to photoprotection, photosynthesis and photorespiration and to pigment, prenylquinone and vitamin metabolism. The FL-induced changes in gene expression varied between young and mature leaves at the same time point, and between the same leaves in the morning and at the end of the day, indicating interactions of FL acclimation with leaf development stage and time of day. Only 46 genes were up- or downregulated in both young and mature leaves at both time points. Combined analyses of gene co-expression and *cis*-elements pointed to a role of the circadian clock and light in coordinating the acclimatory responses of functionally related genes. Our results also suggest a possible crosstalk between FL acclimation and systemic acquired resistance-like gene expression in young leaves.

### 23 INTRODUCTION

Adjustment of photosynthetic light energy utilization and photoprotection to changing light 24 intensity is triggered over different time scales. Rapid changes (seconds to minutes) are induced 25 inside chloroplasts by reduction of electron transport chain or upon formation of a [H<sup>+</sup>] gradient 26 (ΔpH) across the thylakoid membrane. Short-term responses have been studied intensively to 27 unveil various regulatory mechanisms involved therein. Reduction of the plastoquinone (PQ) 28 pool by preferential excitation of photosystem (PS) II relative to PSI, for example, leads to 29 activation of thylakoid protein kinase STN7, which phosphorylates PSII light-harvesting complex 30 31 (LHC) to trigger its displacement from PSI to PSI in a process termed state transition (Bellafiore et al., 2005). Light-dependent acidification of thylakoid lumen protonates the PSBS protein to 32 33 quickly induce thermal energy dissipation (or non-photochemical quenching, NPQ) and thus downregulate PSII (Li et al., 2000). Lumen acidification also activates a xanthophyll-cycle 34 enzyme-violaxanthin de-epoxidase (VDE)-to convert violaxanthin to antheraxanthin and 35 36 zeaxanthin, which enhances NPQ (Niyogi et al., 1998). Furthermore, alternative electron (e<sup>-</sup>) 37 flows, e.g. water-water cycle and cyclic e flow (CEF) around PSI, by which electrons are 38 transferred from PSI to  $H_2O$  through Mehler-ascorbate peroxidase reactions (Asada, 1999) or from ferredoxin (FD) back to PQ (Johnson, 2011; Yamori and Shikanai, 2016), can support  $\Delta pH$ 39 40 formation and hence ATP synthesis and NPQ. In the chloroplast stroma, the thioredoxin (TRX) system mediates light- and redox-dependent regulation of enzyme activities, including those in 41 the Calvin-Benson cycle (CBC) and oxidative pentose phosphate pathway or ATP and starch 42 synthesis (Schürmann and Buchanan, 2008). 43

Long-term changes in growth light environment, on the other hand, elicit acclimatory 44 responses which are slowly inducible and reversible (hours to days) or irreversible. Inside 45 chloroplasts, the relative abundance of photosynthetic protein complexes changes in response 46 47 to prevailing growth light intensity. High-light (HL) and sunlit conditions favor accumulation of PSII, cytochrome  $b_6 f$  (Cyt  $b_6 f$ ) and ATP synthase but suppress LHCII (major LHC of PSII) 48 compared to low-light (LL) and shaded conditions (Evans, 1987; Anderson et al., 1988). In 49 addition, HL and excess light (EL) increase leaf carotenoid content, especially the xanthophyll-50 51 cycle components, along with other antioxidants like tocopherols, ascorbate and glutathione 52 (Demmig-Adams and Adams, 1992: Grace and Logan, 1996; Bartoli et al., 2006; Lichtenthaler, 2007; Matsubara et al., 2009). Together, these biochemical modifications contribute to 53 54 enhancement of photosynthetic e<sup>-</sup> transport, CO<sub>2</sub> assimilation, NPQ and ROS scavenging in HL and EL. 55

Unlike locally controlled short-term responses, long-term acclimation can be observed in both 56 local and distant tissues. Elongation growth of palisade cells is locally controlled via UV-A and 57 blue-light photoreceptor phototropins (Kozuka et al., 2011), which also regulate short-term 58 responses of chloroplast movement and stomatal opening (Christie et al., 2018). In contrast, 59 60 formation of extra palisade cell layers has been seen in leaf primordia and young growing leaves 61 when mature leaves are exposed to HL (Terashima et al., 2006). Changes in these anatomical 62 traits result in leaf thickening and enlargement of palisade cell surface area to facilitate 63 chloroplast CO<sub>2</sub> uptake in HL (Terashima et al., 2006). Additionally, thick leaves can accumulate more photosynthetic enzymes per unit leaf area, which also contributes to greater CO<sub>2</sub> fixation 64 capacity of HL-grown leaves per unit leaf area (but not necessarily per unit leaf mass) (Givnish, 65 1988; Evans and Poorter, 2001). As for photoprotection, systemic acquired acclimation has been 66 reported in young leaves when mature leaves are exposed to EL and HL (Karpinski et al., 1999). 67 Since light and carbon are not limiting for plant growth in HL, whole-plant biomass allocation 68 shifts towards roots to ensure water and nutrient uptake (Poorter et al., 2012). 69

70 Fluctuating growth light environments induce long-term acclimatory responses that are distinct from those found in LL or HL. Acclimatory responses to fluctuating light (FL) are 71 influenced by duration, frequency and amplitude of fluctuations. When LL-grown (100 µmol 72 photons m<sup>-2</sup> s<sup>-1</sup>) plants of Arabidopsis thaliana were transferred to conditions in which light 73 intensity was fluctuating between 100 and 475 or between 100 and 810 µmol photons m<sup>-2</sup> s<sup>-1</sup> 74 every 15 min, 1 h or 3 h during the daytime, O<sub>2</sub> evolution capacity of PSII increased in all FL 75 conditions compared to LL (Yin and Johnson, 2000). Yet, the efficacy to induce this upregulation 76 77 varied in different FL conditions; 3-h cycle was the most effective regime in 100/475 µmol photons  $m^{-2}$  s<sup>-1</sup>, whereas a 15-min cycle allowed the largest increase in O<sub>2</sub> evolution under 78 100/810 µmol photons m<sup>-2</sup> s<sup>-1</sup> (Yin and Johnson, 2000). In marked contrast, Arabidopsis plants 79 80 grown under FL, which consisted of short (20-s) pulses of HL (650 or 1250  $\mu$ mol photons m<sup>-2</sup> s<sup>-1</sup>) applied every 6 or 12 min under LL (50 µmol photons m<sup>-2</sup> s<sup>-1</sup>), upregulated the capacities for 81 NPQ and ROS (reactive oxygen species) scavenging while reducing the PSII efficiency, 82 chlorophyll (Chl) and carbohydrate contents as well as leaf growth (Alter et al., 2012). In 83 conditions mimicking natural light fluctuations, Arabidopsis leaves became thinner than in fixed 84 intensity (constant light, CL) of the same average intensity and day length, while maintaining 85 similar CO<sub>2</sub> fixation rates per unit leaf area (Vialet-Chabrand et al., 2017). Generally, however, 86 responses to FL (and also HL and LL) are dependent on species, pre-experimental conditions 87 and other environmental, physiological and developmental factors (Chazdon and Pearcy, 1986; 88 Tinoco-Ojanguren and Pearcy, 1992; Watling et al., 1997a, b; Yin and Johnson, 2000; Leakey et 89 al., 2003; Kaiser et al., 2018a). 90

 Unraveling molecular mechanisms (or networks) of long-term acclimation is hampered by the complexity arising from a mixture of interlinked or unrelated reactions which are induced concurrently or sequentially over time. Nonetheless, studies in the past ~20 years have led to identification of signaling agents and pathway components involved in acclimation to growth light environments, including EL and photo-oxidative stress. Energy transfer from Chl to O<sup>2</sup> generates singlet oxygen (<sup>1</sup> O2) especially in PSII. The core complexes of PSII and PSI bind β-carotene molecules which can be oxidized by (and thereby detoxify) <sup>1</sup> O2. It has been proposed that some apocarotenoids, which are derived from oxidative cleavage of β-carotene, may serve as stress signals to elicit EL acclimation; when applied to non-stressed leaves of Arabidopsis, an apocarotenoid volatile (β-cyclocitral) was able to induce expression of <sup>1</sup> O2-responsive genes (Ramel et al., 2012; Havaux, 2013). Another stress signal originates from the plastidic methylerythritol phosphate (MEP) pathway, which synthesizes isoprenoid precursors; a MEP pathway intermediate, 2-*C*-methyl-D-erythritol-2,4-cyclodiphosphate (MEcPP), is able to activate the transcription of stress-responsive genes, e.g. *ISOCHORISMATE SYNTHASE 1* (*ICS1*, alias *SALICYLIC ACID INDUCTION DEFICIENT 2*, *SID2*), which encodes an enzyme in salicylic acid (SA) biosynthesis (Xiao et al., 2012; de Souza et al., 2017). Under HL or drought stress, leaves accumulate 3'-phosphoadenosine 5'-phosphate (PAP), a by-product of sulfotransferase reactions to make sulfated compounds; it is thought that PAP inhibits 5'-3' exoribonuclease activity to modulate HL- and oxidative stress-related gene expression (Estavillo et al., 2011; Chan et al., 2016).

 The molecular mechanisms of long-term acclimation to growth light intensity have been studied by transferring plants from constant LL to constant HL (or EL) and *vice versa*. Long-term acclimation to fluctuating growth light intensity has rarely been investigated even though FL comprises a substantial part of daily light exposure for inner-canopy leaves and understory plants (Pearcy, 1990; Kaiser et al., 2018b; Slattery et al., 2018). In addition, for sunlit leaves, sunlight intensity fluctuates as clouds travel and the weather changes. Long-term mechanisms of FL acclimation are therefore of great relevance to understanding how plants respond to growth light conditions in the field. Here we conducted transcriptome profiling in Arabidopsis leaves, focusing on long-term acclimation to the FL conditions which primarily elicit EL and photo-oxidative stress responses (Alter et al., 2012). In particular, we asked whether acclimatory adjustment of gene expression varies between growing (Young) and non-growing (Mature) leaves because stress signaling and acclimation (biochemical, morphological or developmental) may differ between these leaves (Chan et al., 2016). Given the pronounced diurnal variations in gene expression in leaves (Bläsing et al., 2005; Michael et al., 2008b), we also investigated time-of-day-dependent gene expression responses to FL by analyzing leaf transcriptome in the

morning (MO, 1 h after the light was turned on) and at the end of the day (EOD, 1 h before the light was turned off). The results highlight global reprogramming of gene expression during longterm acclimation to FL and indicate strong interactions with both leaf development stage and time of day. The acclimatory responses of functionally related genes seem to be coordinated by circadian clock and light signaling in leaves. Our data also suggest a possible crosstalk between

- 131 FL acclimation and systemic acquired resistance (SAR)-like gene expression in Young leaves.
- 132

### 133 **RESULTS**

### 134 The FL Treatment Induced Photo-oxidative Stress Responses

After germination and cultivation in a CL condition (75 µmol photons m<sup>-2</sup> s<sup>-1</sup> during the light 135 period of 12 h/12 h light/dark) for ca. four weeks, half of the plants (Columbia-0, Col-0) were 136 transferred to a corner of the same climate chamber where the automatically moving LED device 137 (Alter et al., 2012) was creating light fluctuations between 75 (the CL background, ~280 s) and 138 1000  $\mu$ mol photons m<sup>-2</sup> s<sup>-1</sup> (~20 s) during the light period. The other half was kept under CL. 139 140 Based on our previous studies, which had shown acclimatory enhancement of photoprotective 141 capacities in Arabidopsis following 3-d exposure to similar FL conditions (Alter et al., 2012; 142 Caliandro et al., 2013), we took leaf samples for RNA sequencing (RNA-Seq) after 3 d under FL or CL. 143

To directly compare gene expression and phenotypic changes, we characterized 144 physiological and metabolic alterations in leaves during the FL treatment. The plants were 145 visually indistinguishable after growing under FL or CL for 3 d (Fig. 1A). Figure 1B shows Young 146 and Mature leaves used for different analyses. Although there was no visible sign of stress on 147 day 3, the maximal PSII efficiency  $(F_v/F_m)$  was slightly reduced in both Young and Mature leaves 148 under FL compared to CL (Fig. 2A). As in the previous studies (Alter et al., 2012; Caliandro et al., 149 150 2013), rapid light response curves of relative electron transport rate (ETR), which were measured in dark-adapted plants during exposure to stepwise increase in light intensity (20 s at 151 each light level) on day 3, confirmed lower PSII activity and increased NPQ capacity in the FL 152 plants (Fig. 2B and C). When the leaves were allowed to reach steady state in saturating light 153 intensity (800  $\mu$ mol photons m<sup>-2</sup> s<sup>-1</sup>), however, the J<sub>max</sub> and V<sub>cmax</sub> obtained from CO<sub>2</sub> response 154 curves (A/C<sub>i</sub> curves; 4-5 min at each CO<sub>2</sub> level) indicated no change in the capacities for 155 156 ribulose-1,5-bisphosphate (RuBP) regeneration and Rubisco carboxylation in Mature leaves 157 after 3 d under FL (Fig. 2D). Nevertheless, leaf growth suppression in the FL condition became 158 gradually evident between day 3 and day 7 (Fig. 2E). The plants under FL conditions had 16%

 less rosette dry weight than those under CL conditions on day 7 (Fig. 2F) due to reduced leaf expansion (Fig. 2E) and dry mass accumulation per unit leaf area (Fig. 2G).

 Consistent with the growth reduction under FL, the levels of Suc and starch (Fig. 3A, B) and all free amino acids except Glu and Asn at EOD (Supplemental Figure S1) tended to decline in leaves on day 3. While FL did not affect Chl content by day 3 (Fig. 3C), it enhanced carotenoid accumulation (Fig. 3D), especially the xanthophyll-cycle pigments (Fig. 3E). These phenotypic changes (Figs. 2, 3) confirm our previous finding that short HL pulses of FL, regardless of whether or not they increase the average light intensity compared to CL, do not promote growth but trigger EL and photo-oxidative stress in LL-grown Arabidopsis (Alter et al., 2012).

 Long-term effects of FL on the metabolome were also analyzed in Mature leaves on day 7 (Supplemental Table S1). The results corroborated the reduced accumulation of sugars and amino acids under FL especially in MO, while the levels of other metabolites, e.g. mannitol-1- phosphate, glycerate, phytol, ketolutein, ginkgolide and theophylline, significantly increased. The levels of arbutin, a glycosylated hydroquinone which interacts with membrane lipids and acts as an antioxidant, also increased. At EOD, the leaves under FL had larger amounts of organic acids and glycerolipids with polyunsaturated fatty acids (18:3) than those under CL. Another striking change under FL was the enhanced accumulation of vitamin C-related compounds (ascorbate, dehydroascorbate, threonate, gulonate and ketogulonolactone). Collectively, the metabolic alterations under FL can be summarized as increased antioxidant and lipid metabolism with less accumulation of sugars, starch and amino acids, providing further support to the above-described photoprotective and photo-oxidative stress responses and growth inhibition.

# **Leaf Transcriptome Profiles of Long-term FL Acclimation**

 Changes in gene expression were studied in Young and Mature leaves harvested in MO and at EOD of day 3 (hereafter Young-MO, Young-EOD, Mature-MO and Mature-EOD). The RNA- Seq data were filtered by counts per million (CPM >3) and false discovery rate (FDR <0.01). Nearly all genes that satisfied these criteria showed greater than ±30% changes in expression under FL (FL/CL ≥1.3 or ≤0.7; Supplemental Figure S2). Since our FL treatment did not induce severe stress symptoms, we set the threshold at ±30% to take into account strongly expressed genes (e.g. photosynthetic genes) which may not undergo large relative variations.

 The results of RNA-Seq analysis were validated by reverse-transcription quantitative PCR (RT-qPCR) in the same samples. Four genes were selected to cover a wide range of fold change: *LHCB1.2* (*PSII LIGHT HARVESTING COMPLEX 1.2*), *GPX7* (*GLUTATHIONE PEROXIDASE 7*), *ELIP2* (*EARLY LIGHT-INDUCED PROTEIN 2*) and *PR2* (*PATHOGENESIS-RELATED 2*). The RT-qPCR data quantitatively confirmed the fold-change values of RNA-Seq

 (Supplemental Figure S3), with a single outlier (*ELIP2* in Mature-EOD) for which RNA-Seq gave a higher value.

 Figure 4 illustrates the numbers of differentially expressed (DE) genes under FL compared to CL. More genes were upregulated than downregulated after a 3-d FL exposure (Fig. 4A). Many DE genes were shared by Young and Mature leaves in MO, whereas their gene expression responses to FL differed remarkably at EOD (Fig. 4B). In fact, Young-EOD and Mature-EOD had, respectively, the smallest and the largest number of DE genes (Fig. 4A). Neither showed a large overlap of the DE genes between MO and EOD (Fig. 4B). No more than 46 genes were significantly up- or downregulated in both Young and Mature leaves at both time points (Fig. 4C). Lists of all DE genes are provided in Supplemental Data.

 The genes upregulated under FL were overrepresented by components of chloroplasts, whereas genes associated with DNA, ribosome, Golgi apparatus and cell wall were enriched in the downregulated genes (Supplemental Table S2). Some chloroplast components were also enriched in the downregulated genes of Young-EOD. With regard to the biological processes of the DE genes, responses to light stimuli and vitamin metabolism were overrepresented in the upregulated genes of Young and Mature leaves in MO, while photosynthesis and pigment metabolism were overrepresented in the upregulated genes of Mature leaves at both time points (Supplemental Table S3). With regard to the downregulated genes, enrichment was found for DNA replication, cell cycle, organelle fission and organization in Young-MO, and for light harvesting and responses to light and other stimuli in Young-EOD.

## **Genes that were Up- or Downregulated in All FL Samples**

 Figure 5 shows the 46 genes that were differentially expressed in all FL samples. Only three genes were downregulated and the rest were upregulated. Among the upregulated genes was *LHCB7*, which is typically expressed in HL and EL (e.g. Kleine et al., 2007). Three CBC genes also underwent upregulation in all FL samples (Fig. 5): one coding for CP12 protein (CP12-2), which mediates TRX-dependent reversible complex formation between phosphoribulokinase (PRK) and glyceraldehyde-3-phosphate dehydrogenase (GAPDH), one for SEDOHEPTULOSE- 1,7-BISPHOSPHATASE (SBPASE) which, like PRK and GAPDH, is activated by reduced TRX in the light, and one for chloroplast FRUCTOSE-1,6-BISPHOSPHATE ALDOLASE 1 (FBA1), which provides substrates for SBPASE and fructose-1,6-bisphosphatase (FBPASE).

 Oxygenation of RuBP by Rubisco triggers photorespiration in C3 plants. Three genes encoding photorespiratory enzymes were upregulated in all FL samples: GLYCOLATE OXIDASE 1 (GOX1), which produces H2O<sup>2</sup> in peroxisomes; CATALASE 2 (CAT2), which detoxifies  $H_2O_2$  therein; and the P protein of GLYCINE DECARBOXYLASE (GLDP1), which releases  $CO_2$  in mitochondria (Fig. 5). In contrast to *CAT2*, *CAT3* was repressed under FL (Fig. 5). Increased gene expression was also found for BETA CARBONIC ANHYDRASE 1 (BCA1), which interconverts  $CO_2$  and  $HCO_3^-$  in chloroplasts, and SUCROSE-PHOSPHATE SYNTHASE C (SPSC), the second most abundant leaf SPS isoform which catalyzes a rate-limiting step in Suc synthesis.

234 The FL condition promoted the expression of three ACTIVITY OF BC1 COMPLEX KINASE 235 genes (Fig. 5) encoding ABC1K1, ABC1K5 and ABC1K6, all of which are localized in plastoglobuli. The ABC1K1 phosphorylates tocopherol cyclase VITAMIN E 1 (VTE1) in 236 plastoglobuli (Martinis et al., 2014). In parallel, two FATTY ACID DESATURASE genes FAD4 237 238 and FAD8 were also upregulated in all samples (Fig. 5). In chloroplasts, FAD4 provides trans- $\Delta^3$ -hexadecenoic acid (*trans*-16:1) for phosphatidylglycerol (PG) and FAD8 synthesizes  $\alpha$ -239 linolenic acid (18:3  $\omega$ -3) for all glycerolipids but especially PG (Román et al., 2015). When 240 liberated from membrane lipids,  $\alpha$ -linolenic acid can be converted to 12-oxophytodienoic acid 241 242 and jasmonic acid (JA), both of which can elicit defense responses in plants. The products of 243 three MES (METHYL ESTERASE) genes, which were upregulated under FL (Fig. 5), can demethylate methyl JA (MeJA; MES16), methyl indole-3-acetic acid (MeIAA; MES16 and 244 245 MES18) and *p*-nitrophenyl acetate (MES8) in vitro. In vivo MES16 demethylates Chl catabolites 246 (Christ et al., 2012).

247 Concomitant with the accumulation of antioxidants (Supplemental Table S1; Fig. 3D, E), two pyridoxine (vitamin B<sub>6</sub>) biosynthetic genes, PDX1.1 (PYRIDOXAL-5'-PHOSPHATE SYNTHASE 248 249 1.1) and PDX1L4 (PDX1-LIKE 4), were upregulated in leaves under FL (Fig. 5). Pyridoxal 5'phosphate (active form of pyridoxine) is a cofactor of various enzymatic reactions, including 250 251 photorespiratory Gly decarboxylation and the last step of Cys biosynthesis needed for the 252 formation of glutathione, protein disulfide bonds and iron-sulfur proteins. Pyridoxine and its derivatives are also able to scavenge <sup>1</sup>O<sub>2</sub>. All FL samples showed more than fourfold 253 upregulation of *GPX7* (Fig. 5). Plant GPX catalyzes reduction of H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> and lipid hydroperoxide to 254 H<sub>2</sub>O and alcohol, respectively, using reduced TRX or glutathione as an e<sup>-</sup> donor. Arabidopsis 255 has two chloroplast GPX isoforms, GPX1 and GPX7, which play partially redundant roles in 256 257 photo-oxidative stress (Chang et al., 2009).

*BLUE-LIGHT INHIBITOR OF CRYPTOCHROMES 1* (*BIC1*; Fig. 5) encodes a protein that inhibits light-induced dimerization and activation of blue light and UV-A receptors cryptochromes (CRYs). In the nucleus, CRYs interact with COP1/SPA1 ubiquitin ligase to suppress COP1dependent degradation of light response-related transcription factors HY5 (LONG HYPOCOTYL 5) and HYH (HY5 HOMOLOG). Hetero- or homodimers of HY5 and HYH then bind to *cis*-acting

 DNA elements to regulate the transcription of photomorphogenic and light-responsive genes, including *BIC1* and *BIC2* (Wang et al., 2017). The FL samples had higher transcript levels of *HYH* (Fig. 5), which is functionally similar to, but not identical with, *HY5* (Brown and Jenkins, 2008). Conversely, a B-box transcription factor gene, *BBX17*, was downregulated in all samples in the FL condition (Fig. 5). Some members of the BBX family proteins interact with COP1, HY5 and HYH to regulate photomorphogenesis, while others are associated with flowering control and stress responses (Gangappa and Botto, 2014).

 The DE genes in Fig. 5 suggest adjustments of various cellular processes during long-term acclimation to the FL condition. Yet, these are only a small fraction of the DE genes identified under FL (Fig. 4C). In the following sections, we explore genes that were significantly up- or downregulated in not all but some of the FL samples (but always with statistical significance based on the biological replicates), focusing on highly upregulated genes (FL/CL ≥4) and genes that are related to photosynthesis and pigment metabolism.

### **Highly Upregulated Genes under FL**

 Only <30 genes were upregulated by fourfold or greater in any of the FL samples on day 3 (*cf*. Supplemental Figure S2). Since some of these genes were associated with biotic stress responses, we checked their expression in publicly available Arabidopsis leaf transcriptome datasets studying biotic stress and hormone responses. Nine genes, which were highly upregulated under FL but not found in the public datasets, are listed in Supplemental Table S4.

 The expression patterns of our highly upregulated genes revealed a clear contrast between Young and Mature leaves in terms of their responses to biotic stress and hormone treatments (Supplemental Figure S4). About half of the genes, which were highly upregulated in Young leaves under FL (the first three columns from the left; Supplemental Figure S4A, B), were also strongly induced in systemic (non-treated) leaves 2 d after inoculation with *Pseudomonas syringae* pv. *maculicola* (*Psm*) (SAR 48 h; the 4 th column) and in the constitutive SAR mutant *cpr5* (*constitutive expressor of pathogenesis related genes 5*; the 5 th column). The highly upregulated genes of Mature leaves (Supplemental Figure S4C, D), on the other hand, did not respond to SAR. To evaluate the similarity of gene expression, a mean fold change of the highly upregulated genes was calculated for each dataset (i.e., for each column of Supplemental Figure S4) and summarized in Fig. 6. The SAR 48 h and *cpr5*/Col datasets had very high mean fold-change values (>10- or 20-fold) for the highly upregulated genes of Young leaves. While high mean values (>4-fold) were also found for the genes of Young-EOD in the JA-dependent response to *Psm* (Col*/coi1, coronate insensitive 1*) and the SA 2 h or MeJA 3 h treatment, these were attributable to very strong upregulation of a few genes (Supplemental Figure S4B).

Since SA is a major endogenous signal for SAR induction and since SA accumulation is also 298 299 associated with plastid-to-nucleus retrograde signaling via MEcPP or  $\beta$ -cyclocitral (Xiao et al., 300 2012; Lv et al., 2015), we analyzed SA contents in leaves on day 3. Low levels of SA and SA 301 glucosides were detected under both FL and CL conditions, with twice as much SA found in 302 Young leaves as in Mature leaves (Supplemental Figure S5A, B). In contrast to the responses of 303 SAR-like genes in Young leaves, however, the levels of SA and SA glucosides were unchanged 304 or even reduced under FL. Pipecolic acid (Pip), an L-Lys derived non-protein amino acid (Hartmann et al., 2017), is another SAR regulatory metabolite that accumulates in response to 305 306 pathogen inoculation (Návarová et al., 2012). Unlike in the case of SA, FL tended to promote Pip accumulation in both young and mature leaves but especially the latter (Supplemental Figure 307 S5C). The levels of JA were below detection limit in all samples. 308

309

# 310 Differentially Regulated Genes in Photoprotection, Photosynthesis and Photorespiration

311 As shown by the enrichment of photosynthesis and chloroplast components in the DE genes (Supplemental Tables S2, S3), a large number of photoprotective, photosynthetic and 312 313 photorespiratory genes were responding to FL in different samples (Fig. 7). Most of these genes were upregulated under FL, with the exception of *LHCBs* and a gene for a 5-kD PSII protein, 314 315 which were downregulated in Young-EOD. All but Young-EOD had increased transcript levels of PSBS and KEA1 ( $K^{+}$  EFFLUX ANTIPORTER 1), which encodes a  $K^{+}/H^{+}$  antiporter in the 316 317 chloroplast inner envelop. As for the thylakoid-localized KEA3, gene expression was enhanced 318 in Mature leaves at both time points (Fig. 7). Upregulation under FL was also found for ELIP1 319 and ELIP2 especially in MO.

320 As the decrease in linear e transport and increase in NPQ were detected in the FL plants (Fig. 2B, C), genes related to CEF underwent concomitant upregulation in Mature-EOD (Fig. 7). Many 321 322 genes associated with NAD(P)H dehydrogenase (NDH)-like complex were upregulated in concert, together with LHCA5 and LHCA6, which are needed for the formation of the NDH-PSI 323 supercomplex. Also, components of the other CEF pathway—PGR5 (PROTON GRADIENT 324 REGULATION 5), PGRL1A (PGR5-LIKE 1A) and PGRL1B—showed increased gene expression 325 in Mature-EOD. Upregulated in Mature-EOD were also Rieske iron-sulfur protein gene PETC 326 (PHOTOSYNTHETIC ELECTRON TRANSFER C) and PETM of Cyt b<sub>6</sub>f, ATP synthase subunit 327 genes ATPC1 and PDE334 (PIGMENT DEFECTIVE 334) as well as the assembly component 328 329 CGL160 (CONSERVED ONLY IN THE GREEN LINEAGE 160) (Fig. 7). Furthermore, the FL treatment enhanced the gene expression of STN7 and CHLOROPLAST SENSOR KINASE 330

(CSK) which, in response to the PQ pool reduction, facilitate balancing of PSII and PSI excitation
 via LHCII phosphorylation and gene transcription.

The iron-sulfur protein FD mediates e<sup>-</sup> transfer from PSI to FD:NADP<sup>+</sup> REDUCTASE (FNR), 333 CEF and various chloroplast redox and metabolic pathways. Both minor (FD1) and major (FD2) 334 335 leaf-type FD genes of Arabidopsis were upregulated under FL but at different time points (Fig. 7). 336 Further, Mature-EOD had increased expression of leaf FNR (FNR1 and FNR2), FTR (FD:TRX 337 REDUCTASE) and multiple chloroplast TRX genes, including ACHT4 (ATYPICAL CYS HIS 338 RICH TRX 4). The stroma thylakoid-localized ACHT4, together with 2-Cys peroxiredoxin, 339 transduces oxidative signals to inactivate the first enzyme of starch synthesis and thereby counteracts reductive activation signals from other TRXs (Geigenberger et al., 2017). The redox 340 341 signals thus transduced are thought to fine-tune the enzyme activity under fluctuating light conditions. 342

While the maximum capacity of RuBP regeneration  $(J_{max})$  and Rubisco carboxylation  $(V_{cmax})$ 343 did not change (Fig. 2D), genes in the CBC manifested parallel upregulation in Mature leaves 344 345 after 3 d under FL. Starting from Rubisco small subunits (RBCS) and RUBISCO ACTIVASE (RCA), all CBC enzymes except triose phosphate isomerase are found in Fig. 7. Marked co-346 upregulation under FL was also seen for photorespiratory genes, including GOGAT 347 (GLUTAMINE: OXOGLUTARATE AMINOTRANSFERASE), GS2 (GLUTAMINE SYNTHETASE 348 2) and its activator ACR11 (ACT DOMAIN REPEATS 11) for NH<sub>3</sub> re-assimilation. Besides, three 349 samples had increased transcript levels of BCA1 and BCA4 (Fig. 7); BCA1 in chloroplasts and 350 BCA4 in the plasma membrane are thought to co-regulate intracellular [HCO<sub>3</sub>] and CO<sub>2</sub>-induced 351 352 guard cell movements (Hu et al., 2015).

353

# **Differentially Regulated Genes in Pigment and Prenylquinone Metabolism**

The overrepresentation of pigment metabolism in the DE genes of Mature leaves (Supplemental Table S3) prompted us to examine gene expression responses in tetrapyrrole, carotenoid and prenylquinone metabolism. Six genes in the initial part of tetrapyrrole biosynthesis were upregulated in Mature-EOD (Fig. 8), including *GLUTR1*, which codes for redox-regulated GLUTAMYL TRNA REDUCTASE at the first committed step of tetrapyrrole biosynthesis and *GLUTRBP* for thylakoid-localized GLUTR-BINDING PROTEIN.

In the Chl biosynthetic pathway, *NADPH: PROTOCHLOROPHYLLIDE OXIDOREDUCTASE A (PORA)* and *PORB* were strongly downregulated in the FL condition (Fig. 8). Arabidopsis has three POR isozymes that catalyze light-dependent reduction of protochlorophyllide to chlorophyllide *a*; PORA and PORB are active during early stages of greening, whereas PORB and PORC may maintain Chl in green leaves (Frick et al., 2003; Masuda et al., 2003). In

 contrast to *PORA* and *PORB*, *PORC* showed enhanced expression under FL in Mature-EOD, together with several other genes in Chl biosynthesis, e.g. *GUN5* (*GENOMES UNCOUPLED 5*), which encodes H subunit of Mg chelatase associated with retrograde signaling (Mochizuki et al. 2001). Although the plants were similarly green under FL and CL on day 3 (Figs. 1A, 3C), longer FL exposure reduces Chl contents (Alter et al., 2012; Caliandro et al., 2013). In accordance, we found concomitant upregulation of Chl breakdown genes, *NYC1* (*NON-YELLOW COLORING 1*), *SGR1* (*STAY-GREEN 1*), *CLD1* (*CHLOROPHYLL DEPHYTYLASE 1*), *PES1* (*PHYTYL ESTER SYNTHASE 1*), *RCCR* (*RED CHLOROPHYLL CATABOLITE REDUCTASE*), *TIC55* (*TRANSLOCON AT THE INNER ENVELOPE MEMBRANE OF CHLOROPLASTS 55*) and *MES16* (Fig. 8).

 FERROCHELATASE 2 (FC2) is involved in the synthesis of heme-containing Cyt *b*6*f*. Since heme harbors redox-active Fe, which can produce highly reactive hydroxyl radicals from H2O2, the accumulation of free heme should be avoided. Chloroplast-localized HEME-BINDING PROTEIN 5 (HBP5) binds heme and interacts with HEME OXYGENASE 1 (HO1) to regulate heme metabolism (Lee et al., 2012). The three genes *FC2*, *HBP5* and *HO1* were upregulated together in Mature-EOD (Fig. 8). The MO samples, on the other hand, had increased transcripts of *HBP1* encoding a putative tetrapyrrole carrier protein found in cytoplasm and around the nucleus.

 Mature leaves showed concerted upregulation of genes in the MEP and carotenoid biosynthetic pathways especially at EOD (Fig. 8). In Arabidopsis, GERANYLGERANYL DIPHOSPHATE SYNTHASE 11 (GGPS11) supports the synthesis of carotenoids and isoprenoid side chains of Chls, PQ and tocopherols (Ruiz-Sola et al., 2016). While Mature leaves had increased transcript levels of *GGPS11* and several carotenogenic genes such as *ZEAXANTHIN EPOXIDASE* (*ZEP*) and *VDE* in the xanthophyll cycle, two *B-CAROTENE HYDROXYLASE* genes *BCH1* and *BCH2*—were the only DE genes of this pathway identified in Young leaves (Fig. 8). The two BCH enzymes primarily catalyze β-ring hydroxylation of β- carotene to produce zeaxanthin. With regard to apocarotenoid metabolism, FL upregulated *B- CAROTENE ISOMERASE* (*DWARF 27*, *D27*), *CCD1* (*CAROTENOID CLEAVAGE DIOXYGENASE 1*) and *CCD4* while repressing two genes in abscisic acid (ABA) and strigolactone biosynthesis at EOD, namely, *ABA2* (*ABA DEFICIENT 2*) in Young leaves and *MAX1* (*MORE AXILLARY BRANCHES 1*) in Mature leaves.

 All DE genes in prenylquinone biosynthesis were upregulated under FL, mostly in MO (Fig. 8). Enhanced gene expression was found for *TAT1* (*TYROSINE AMINOTRANSFERASE 1*) and *HPPD* (*4-HYDROXYPHENYLPYRUVATE DIOXYGENASE*), which are involved in homogentisate biosynthesis for PQ and tocopherols; *SPS1* (*SOLANESYL DIPHOSPHATE* 

*SYNTHASE 1*) and *SPS2*, which provide isoprenoid side chains for ubiquinone and PQ, respectively; and *VTE1* and *VTE4*, which are involved in tocopherol and plastochromanol biosynthesis. Increased accumulation of α-tocopherol, reduced PQ (PQH2) and plastochromanol, all of which can serve as antioxidants, has been reported in Arabidopsis leaves under HL (Dłużewska et al., 2015). We also found upregulation of two phylloquinone (vitamin K1) biosynthetic genes, *PHYLLO* and *ABC4* (*ABERRANT CHLOROPLAST DEVELOPMENT 4*), in Mature-EOD (Fig. 8). Inside PSI, phylloquinone mediates e- transfer from Chl A<sup>0</sup> to the first iron-sulfur cluster Fx.

## **Response of Cyclic Gene Expression to FL**

 The concomitant responses of functionally related DE genes (Figs. 5, 7, 8) point to their co- regulation, whereas the limited overlaps found between the DE genes of Young and Mature leaves in MO and at EOD (Fig. 4) indicate interactions between FL, leaf development stage and diurnal processes. In particular, the distinct FL responses observed in MO and at EOD (Fig. 4B) are suggestive of time-of-day–dependent transcriptional regulation by circadian clock, the molecular time keeper of the cell. Indeed, 38-50% of our DE genes show light/dark cyclic expression in leaves and 42–58% are classified as circadian-regulated since they maintain cyclic expression in continuous light (light/light) (Fig. 9A; see Supplemental Data for DE cyclic genes). Interestingly, most of the light/dark cyclic genes, which were upregulated under FL in MO, are "morning genes" peaking between dawn and midday (Fig. 9B). In clear contrast, those that were downregulated under FL in MO are "evening genes". While the number of the DE cyclic genes was too small to infer peak phase distribution in Young-EOD, the pattern in Mature-EOD was similar to those found in MO, except that there was an additional cluster of upregulated evening genes (Fig. 9B).

 Seeing the opposite FL responses of morning and evening genes, we examined the biological processes of these genes. For this analysis, the DE genes peaking at ZT21-ZT6 were grouped together as morning genes and those peaking at ZT8-ZT16 as evening genes; Zeitgeber time zero (ZT0) is defined as the time when the light is turned on. Many DE cyclic genes are associated with synthesis, modification and degradation of RNAs and proteins (Fig. 9C). Nearly all morning genes in these processes were upregulated under FL, whereas the evening genes were mostly downregulated in MO and upregulated in Mature-EOD. Similar patterns were also found for the genes in carbohydrate and secondary metabolism or redox and signaling. The genes predominantly downregulated under FL were growth-related evening genes involved in processes such as DNA synthesis and organization, cell division, and  development of cytoskeleton and cell wall (Fig. 9C), in keeping with the overrepresentation of these processes in the downregulated genes of Young-MO (Supplemental Table 3).

 Changes in the expression of cyclic genes observed at two particular time points could be caused by (i) altered amplitude of oscillation or (ii) a shift of peak expression time. To determine which of these scenarios can explain the observation for FL, we analyzed day-night (diel) expression of four DE cyclic genes (*LHCB1.2*, *CAT2*, *ELIP2* and *GPX7*) in Young and Mature leaves on day 1 and day 3. Two highly expressed genes in leaves, *LHCB1.2* and *CAT2*, were repressed or enhanced, respectively, under FL on both days (Supplemental Figure S6A-D), confirming qualitatively the transcriptome data (Figs. 5, 7). Their peak expression time seemed to change slightly from day 1 to day 3 but this was observed in both conditions. Two stress- responsive genes, *ELIP2* and *GPX7*, underwent strong upregulation under FL (Supplemental Figure S6E-H), as shown by RNA-Seq (Figs. 5 and 7). While the expression of *ELIP2* declined from day 1 to day 3, it remained high for *GPX7*. Together, the results support altered amplitude of cyclic gene expression under FL.

 Many components of the central clock oscillator were among the DE cyclic genes under FL. Both morning and evening component genes were upregulated in their respective phases, namely, Myb transcription factor genes *CCA1* (*CIRCADIAN CLOCK ASSOCIATED 1*) and *LHY* (*LATE ELONGATED HYPOCOTYL*) in MO and transcriptional repressor genes *PRR7* (*PSEUDO-RESPONSE REGULATOR 7*), *PRR5*, *PRR3* and *TOC1* (*TIMING OF CAB EXPRESSION 1*) together with *ELF3* (*EARLY FLOWERING 3*) and *ELF4* at EOD (Supplemental Figures S6I-L, S7). Three samples had increased transcript levels of light- and stress-responsive gene *SIGMA FACTOR 5* (*SIG5*) encoding a plastid RNA polymerase subunit which regulates transcription of PSII D2 protein gene *psbD*. Although SIG5 is not a component but an output of the clock, its cyclic gene expression coordinates chloroplast transcription with circadian regulation of nuclear transcription (Noordally et al., 2013). We also found upregulation of *SIG4* in Mature-EOD (Supplemental Figure S7), in which many genes of NDH-like complex were concurrently upregulated (Fig. 7); SIG4 regulates transcription of the plastid-encoded NDH-like complex gene *ndhF*.

# *Cis***-regulatory Elements Shared by FL-responsive Cyclic Genes**

 We then asked whether these light/dark cyclic genes, whose expression was changing in concert during the FL treatment, shared conserved DNA sequence motifs in promoter regions. *Cis*-element analysis was performed by using ATCOECIS (Vandepoele et al., 2009). The consensus sequences identified in the promoter regions of these genes were identical with, or very similar to, common plant *cis*-elements (Table 1): I box (TCTTATC, complementary to

GATAAGA), G box (CACGTG) and Z box (ATACGTGT) associated with light response, 470 photosynthesis and circadian regulation, ACGT-containing hexamer (Hex, ACGTCA) and the 471 complementary TGA box (TGACGT), and E2F motifs (TTTCCCGC and the complementary 472 GCGGGAAA) involved in the regulation of cell cycle and DNA replication. While these motifs 473 474 typically appear in gene co-expression analysis (Vandepoele et al., 2009), their enrichment 475 patterns differed between our samples. The promoter regions of the DE light/dark cyclic genes in 476 all samples were enriched in I box-like CTTATCC; MO, in two G box variants (CACGTGGC and 477 CACGTGTA); Young leaves, in a Z box-like TACGTGGA; and Young-MO and Mature-EOD in 478 E2F-like motifs. The Hex and TGA box were overrepresented in all but Young-EOD (Table I). The distinct motif enrichment patterns found in the four samples add further support to the 479 480 interactions between FL, leaf development stage and time of day in acclimatory regulation of gene expression. 481

482

### 483 **DISCUSSION**

### 484 FL Upregulates Genes for Protection against EL and Photo-oxidative Stress

Under fluctuating irradiance, light reactions in thylakoids respond almost instantaneously 485 while the rate of  $CO_2$  fixation changes more slowly, limited by the activation state of enzymes, 486 the availability of CBC intermediates and ATP or slow response of stomata (Pearcy, 1990; 487 488 Kaiser et al., 2018b; Slattery et al., 2018). When the light intensity drops from HL to LL, 489 "assimilatory charge" (Laisk et al., 1984), which may be built up in  $C_3$  plants under HL, continues 490 to fuel the CBC in LL, resulting in a somewhat delayed decline of CO<sub>2</sub> uptake. Conversely, the slow response of CO<sub>2</sub> fixation upon LL-to-HL transition leads to EL absorption and excess e that 491 can be transferred to  $O_2$  to form  ${}^1O_2$  and superoxide, respectively. Downregulating PSII and 492 linear e<sup>-</sup> transport on the one hand and scavenging excess e<sup>-</sup> and ROS on the other hand are 493 494 essential to mitigate photo-oxidative damage in such conditions.

Leaves of rainforest understory plants rapidly induce NPQ and accumulate zeaxanthin during 495 sunflecks (Logan et al., 1997; Watling et al., 1997b). In the long term, frequent sunfleck 496 exposure leads to pool size enlargement of the xanthophyll-cycle pigments (Logan et al., 1997). 497 498 Likewise, FL regimes, which are similar to the FL condition in this study, enhance photoprotective capacities in Arabidopsis leaves, as manifested by a parallel increase in the 499 500 maximal NPQ, the pool size of the xanthophyll-cycle pigments and PSBS protein content (Alter 501 et al., 2012; Caliandro et al., 2013). In the present study, in Young and Mature leaves, PSBS, BCH1 and BCH2 were upregulated (Figs. 7, 8), which can positively affect NPQ and the 502 xanthophyll-cycle pool size (Davison et al., 2002; Li et al., 2002). In addition, Mature-EOD had 503

increased gene expression of ZEP and VDE (Fig. 7). It has been reported that tobacco plants 504 co-overexpressing *PSBS*, *ZEP* and *VDE* achieved higher CO<sub>2</sub> assimilation under FL and ~15% 505 more dry matter production in the field than the wild type (Kromdijk et al., 2016). The improved 506 507 performance of the transgenic plants was ascribed to their ability to guickly adjust the levels of 508 zeaxanthin and NPQ under FL (Kromdijk et al., 2016) since slow NPQ relaxation following HL-to-509 LL transition is predicted to cause losses in carbon gain (Zhu et al., 2004). Rapid NPQ relaxation 510 may also be facilitated by the thylakoid  $H^+/K^+$  antiporter KEA3 (Fig. 7) which releases  $H^+$  from 511 the lumen and thereby dissipates  $\Delta pH$  to deactivate PSBS and VDE (thus NPQ) while increasing 512 the contribution of the electric component of proton motive force for ATP synthesis (Armbruster et al., 2014, 2016). Concomitant upregulation of these genes (Figs. 5, 7) is in accordance with 513 514 the necessity for efficient NPQ regulation under FL.

Notably, our results revealed increased gene expression of both NDH- and PGR5-dependent 515 CEF pathway components under FL, especially in Mature leaves (Fig. 7). The PGR5-dependent 516 CEF protects PSI against excess e<sup>-</sup> by alleviating the acceptor-side limitation and contributing to 517  $\Delta pH$  formation which then downregulates e<sup>-</sup> transport at PSII and Cyt  $b_{\beta}f$  (Munekage et al., 518 519 2002; Kono and Terashima, 2016; Suorsa et al., 2016). The ability to thus adjust e<sup>-</sup> flows is critical in fluctuating conditions, as demonstrated by PSI photoinhibition and ultimate death of 520 pgr5 mutant under FL (Munekage et al., 2002; Suorsa et al., 2012, 2016; Kono and Terashima, 521 2016). In comparison, NDH mutants (crr4-3 and ndho) show only mild symptoms and their 522 growth is hardly affected in CL or FL (Suorsa et al., 2012, 2016). Hence, the contribution of the 523 NDH-dependent CEF is considered minor in  $C_3$  plants under FL (Kono et al., 2014; Kono and 524 525 Terashima, 2016; Suorsa et al., 2016). Nevertheless, the marked co-upregulation of SIG4 526 (Supplemental Figure S7) and NDH-like complex genes in Mature-EOD (Fig. 7), including all 527 genes of nuclear-encoded subunits and protein factors except NDHS (or CRR31), argues for a 528 role of this complex in FL acclimation. Photo-oxidative stress and H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> were shown to induce transcription of plastid-encoded NDH-complex genes in barley leaves (Martín et al., 1996; 529 530 Casano et al., 2001). Also, increased NDH-dependent CEF was measured in Arabidopsis mutants (Strand et al., 2015) which produce high levels of H<sub>2</sub>O<sub>2</sub> in leaves due to chloroplast 531 FBPASE deficiency (*hcef1*; Livingston et al., 2010) or ectopic expression of photorespiratory 532 GOX1 in chloroplasts (Fahnenstich et al., 2008). Accordingly, differential regulation of two CEF 533 pathways has been proposed: the PGR5-dependent pathway regulated by redox change and 534 the NDH-dependent pathway responding to  $H_2O_2$  accumulation (Strand et al., 2016). The results 535 of this study support the engagement of both pathways in long-term acclimation to the FL 536 condition. 537

 The ectopically expressed GOX1 oxidizes glycolate to glyoxylate in the light and thereby produces H2O<sup>2</sup> in chloroplasts (Fahnenstich et al., 2008). As a result, the mutants develop small rosettes with patchy pale-green leaves under photorespiratory LL conditions and bleach under HL. Yet, this pale-green phenotype disappears when the mutants are grown under FL (Matsubara et al., 2016), suggesting an interplay between FL acclimation and H2O<sup>2</sup> scavenging and/or signaling. Reinforcement of H2O<sup>2</sup> detoxification under FL, as indicated by the upregulation of *GPX7* and *CAT2* (Fig. 5; Supplemental Figure S6) and the larger pool of ascorbate (Supplemental Table S1), may help the mutants keep H2O<sup>2</sup> under control.

 The expression of *GPX7* can be induced by HL, UV and blue light via transcription factors HY5 and HYH downstream of photoreceptors UVR8 (UV-B-RESISTANCE 8) and CRY1 (Kleine et al., 2007; Lee et al., 2007; Brown and Jenkins, 2008). These light signaling pathways also affect the expression of other stress-responsive genes we found in FL, such as *ELIPs*, *LHCB7*, *SIG5* and *PDX* (Kleine et al., 2007; Brown and Jenkins, 2008; Onda et al., 2008). The upregulation of *HYH* and *BIC1* (Fig. 5) may imply a role of blue light in acclimation to the FL condition. In fact, Arabidopsis *cry1* mutant is sensitive to HL (Kleine et al., 2007) and a role of CRY1 in *LHCB* downregulation (Ruckle et al., 2007) and <sup>1</sup> O2-dependent programmed cell death (Danon et al., 2006) has been proposed. The mechanisms by which CRY1 controls acclimation to EL and photo-oxidative stress are not yet understood.

### **Why Do Plants Upregulate CBC and Photorespiratory Genes under FL?**

 Given that limitation to CO2 assimilation exacerbates EL and ROS production, photo-oxidative stress could be alleviated by removing limitations in the CBC. The upregulation of CBC genes under FL (Fig. 7) is in line with this notion. Of the three genes (*CP12-2*, *FBA* and *SBPASE*) that were upregulated in all FL samples (Fig. 5), *FBA*, *SBPASE* or both, when overexpressed, have been shown to increase CO<sup>2</sup> assimilation in tobacco and Arabidopsis leaves, consistent with the control of these enzymes over the CBC activity (Lefebvre et al., 2005; Uematsu et al., 2012; Simkin et al., 2015, 2017). Despite the upregulation of these genes, however, Young and Mature leaves had lower ETR (Fig. 2B) and less Suc and starch under FL (Fig. 3A, B). Furthermore, the maximal rates of RuBP regeneration and Rubisco carboxylation were unchanged in Mature leaves after 3 d under FL (Fig. 2D). This agrees with the report that non-growing mature leaves of Col-0 plants did not upregulate the CO2-fixation capacity during long-term acclimation to HL (Athanasiou et al., 2010), whereas leaves grown in HL do (e.g. Vialet-Chabrand et al., 2017). Importantly, leaves of Col-0 did not increase the CO2-fixation capacity when grown under FL conditions (Vialet-Chabrand et al., 2017), suggesting limited efficacy of FL to trigger acclimatory enhancement of CO<sup>2</sup> assimilation. The previous studies in rainforest species also provided little

 or no evidence for enhancement of the area-based photosynthetic capacity in plants grown under FL (Watling et al., 1997a; Leakey et al., 2002). The discrepancy between the responses of CBC gene expression (Fig. 7) and CO<sup>2</sup> fixation (Fig. 2D) found in Mature leaves after 3 d in the FL condition may be explained by translational or post-translational regulation (Kawaguchi et al., 2004; Schürmann and Buchanan 2008; Zaffagnini et al., 2012; Pal et al., 2013; Oelze et al., 2014; Geigenberger et al., 2017). It is also possible that the maximum photosynthesis rate, which is measured at steady state, is not a good indicator of FL acclimation. Under FL, plants may adjust other traits, e.g. enzyme activation (Yamori et al., 2012) or energy storage during transient HL (Laisk et al., 1984).

 While upregulation of photosynthetic genes was more pronounced in Mature leaves, the expression of photorespiratory genes was similarly upregulated in both Young and Mature leaves (Fig. 7). Photorespiration protects photosynthesis against photoinhibition by recycling the carbon of glycollate-2-phosphate and by serving as a sink for ATP and reduced FD (Osmond and Grace, 1995; Wingler et al., 2000; Takahashi et al., 2007). In addition, Gly, which is synthesized in the photorespiratory pathway, supports glutathione accumulation in leaves (Noctor et al., 1999). The balance between RuBP carboxylation and oxygenation shifts towards the latter as [CO2] in the chloroplast stroma declines. Since stomatal conductance responds much more slowly than photochemical and biochemical reactions of photosynthesis upon LL-to- HL transition, the [CO2] inside the leaf transiently drops under FL conditions (Vialet-Chabrand et al., 2017) to promote photorespiration (Huang et al., 2015). Parallel upregulation of photorespiratory genes along with *BCA1* and *BCA4* (Fig. 7) is in agreement with increased photorespiration under FL.

 Leaves grown under FL had lower levels of Gly, Ser and glyoxylate (Supplemental Figure S1; Supplemental Table S1), three photorespiratory metabolites that are implicated in feedback regulation of photorespiratory gene expression and RuBP supply (Wingler et al., 2000; Timm et al., 2016). Only glycerate, which inhibits SBPASE and FBPASE in the RuBP regeneration phase of the CBC (Schimkat et al., 1990), showed 40% or 70% increase under FL (Supplemental Table S1). The regulation of SBPASE and FBPASE activities by redox and glycerate (Schimkat et al., 1990; Schürmann and Buchanan 2008), along with ROS-induced modifications of redox- regulated CBC enzymes (Zaffagnini et al., 2012), may attune metabolic flux in the CBC and photorespiratory pathway under photo-oxidative FL conditions.

### **FL Alters Gene Expression in Pigment and Prenylquinone Metabolism**

 Consistent with the decline in *LHCB* gene expression (Fig 7; Supplemental Figure S6A, B) and Chl content during long-term FL exposure (Alter et al., 2012; Caliandro et al., 2013), we

 found downregulation of *PORA* and *PORB* and upregulation of Chl breakdown genes (Fig. 8). Phytol is released during Chl degradation to be recycled as the side chain of tocopherols or esterified to fatty acids in plastoglobuli (Ischebeck et al., 2006; Lippold et al., 2012; vom Dorp et al., 2015). Mature leaves grown under FL showed 70% or 170% increase in phytol on day 7 (Supplemental Table S1) and both Young and Mature leaves showed upregulation of tocopherol biosynthetic genes on day 3 (Fig. 8) albeit not *VTE5* and *VTE6* encoding phytol kinase and phytyl-phosphate kinase, which mediate the phytol recycling (Valentin et al., 2006; vom Dorp et al., 2015). The parallel upregulation of genes involved in Chl breakdown, prenylquinone and carotenoid biosynthesis as well as *ABC1K*s (Figs. 5, 8) is indicative of increased metabolism and exchange of pigments and prenyllipids in thylakoids and plastoglobuli under FL.

 Mature leaves of the FL plants also accumulated greater amounts of polyunsaturated fatty acids at EOD (Supplemental Table S1) and all FL samples had increased transcript levels of *FAD8* and *FAD4* (Fig. 5) for synthesis of 18:3 ω-3 and *trans*-16:1 especially for PG (Román et al., 2015). Unsaturated fatty acids are prone to peroxidation by ROS. In the lipid bilayer or bound to photosynthetic protein complexes, some unsaturated fatty acids may be oxidized in the FL condition. Tightly bound PG molecules are found in both PSI and PSII cores (Umena et al., 2011; Mazor et al., 2017) and in the monomer-monomer interface of LHCII trimers (Liu et al., 2004). Bound in these complexes are also Chls and carotenoids. Continuous turnover of Chl *a* and β-carotene, presumably as part of PSII D1 repair cycle (Baena-González and Aro, 2002), has been reported in Arabidopsis leaves (Beisel et al., 2010, 2011). The enhanced expression of Chl and carotenoid biosynthetic genes in Mature leaves (Fig. 8), despite the decline in *LHCB* expression and Chl content, may reflect high turnover of these pigments in photo-oxidative FL conditions. It is noteworthy that *PORC*, the only *POR* gene that was upregulated in Mature-EOD under FL (Fig. 8), is HL-inducible and seedlings of PORC-deficient Arabidopsis mutant accumulate <50% of the wild-type Chl level when grown in HL (Masuda et al., 2003). The contrasting FL responses of three *POR* genes suggest separate regulation of Chl biosynthesis for light harvesting and damage repair.

 Compared to the pathways of Chl catabolism and recycling, much less is known about the fate of carotenoids. Oxidation of carotenoids produces various apocarotenoids, including reactive electrophile species (e.g. β-cyclocitral), which react with redox-active compounds, especially thiols, to affect redox signaling and gene transcription (Ramel et al., 2012; Havaux, 2013). Although contributions of non-enzymatic and enzymatic carotenoid cleavage are not yet clarified in leaves, our FL samples showed upregulation of *CCD1* and *CCD4* (Fig. 8). Cleavage by CCD4 is specific to 9,10 (9',10') double bond of cyclic all-*trans* carotenoids and apocarotenoids (Lätari et al., 2015; Bruno et al., 2016). All photosynthetic carotenoids, except 9ʹ-

*cis*-neoxanthin, are potential substrates of CCD4. The cytosolic CCD1 (Auldridge et al., 2006) may then remove oxidation products (non-enzymatic or enzymatic) of carotenoids since it can cleave a range of cyclic and acyclic carotenoids and apocarotenoids at different positions of double bond (Schmidt et al., 2006; Vogel et al., 2008). During leaf senescence β-carotene, lutein and violaxanthin are retained (Biswal, 1995) or cleaved by CCD4 (Rottet et al., 2016) in plastoglobuli. Also in non-senescent leaves CCD4-dependent cleavage of all-*trans* xanthophylls has been detected (Lätari et al., 2015). To what extent pigment and lipid turnover contributes to maintenance and acclimation of thylakoids is a question worth investigating in the future.

### **How is Global Reprograming of Gene Expression Coordinated during FL Acclimation?**

 The persistent oscillation of DE cyclic genes following the transfer from CL to FL (Supplemental Figure S6) demonstrates a fundamental role of diel programs in organizing acclimatory adjustment of gene expression in leaves. Circadian regulation of gene expression is integral to plant stress responses, such as low temperature, UV-B, ROS and pathogens (Dong et al., 2011; Fehér et al., 2011; Wang et al., 2011; Lai et al., 2012; Takeuchi et al., 2014). Due to rhythmic gene expression in leaves, observations based on a single time point may be biased towards genes and responses that are gated at that specific time of day (Bieniawska et al., 2008). The small overlaps found between our DE genes in MO and at EOD (Fig. 4B) underline the necessity to study gene expression response at multiple times of day.

 Plant circadian clock orchestrates gene transcription and synchronizes biological processes with recurring changes in environments (Farré, 2012; Hsu and Harmer, 2014; Greenham and McClung, 2015). Co-expression of photosynthetic genes from morning to midday is a well-known example of clock regulation operating at the transcriptional level (Harmer et al., 2000). The contrasting but coordinated FL responses of morning and evening genes, as found in Fig. 9, invite a question about co-adjustment mechanisms of cyclic gene expression during acclimation. The overrepresentation of G and Z box-like motifs in the promoter regions of the DE light/dark cyclic genes in MO (Table I) corresponds to the enrichment of light stimuli and chloroplast components in the DE genes (Supplemental Tables S2, S3). The role of these motifs is well established for light-dependent regulation of *RBCS* and *LHCB* promoters in combination with I box or I box-like GATA element (Argüello-Astorga and Herrera-Estrella, 1998). A large number of G and Z box elements are targeted by HY5 and PIF3 (PHYTOCHROME INTERACTING FACTOR 3) in the downstream of phytochromes, CRYs and UVR8 during photomorphogenesis and UV-B response (Yadav et al., 2002; Jiao et al., 2007). In principle, FL could modulate light- responsive gene transcription via photoreceptors. We note, however, that our list of DE genes includes only a small fraction of genes having a G or Z box.

 Combinatorial or competitive binding of multiple transcription factors in neighboring DNA regions confers context-dependent complex regulation of gene transcription. In the case of *LHCB1* promoters, for instance, binding of the clock oscillator CCA1 to CCA1-binding sites enhances adjacent G box binding of HY5 and this interaction is modulated by phosphorylation of CCA1 and HY5 (Hardtke et al., 2000; Andronis et al., 2008). Genes regulated by a G box typically exhibit light/dark cyclic expression (Ezer et al., 2017), and conversely, G box motif is enriched in target DNA-binding regions of clock components, CCA1, TOC1, PRR5, PRR7 and PRR9 (Huang et al., 2012; Gendron et al., 2012; Nakamichi et al., 2012; Liu et al., 2013, 2016; Nagel et al., 2015), most of which were upregulated in the FL condition (Supplemental Figure S7). Combinatorial interplay is also well known for ABA-inducible gene promoters having G box- like ACGTGGC together with coupling elements (Shen and Ho, 1995) or auxin-inducible soybean *GH3* promoter having Hex/TGA boxes next to auxin-responsive elements (Ulmasov et al., 1995). The TGA box TGACGT and Hex CCACGTCA are similar (or complementary) to stress-induced unfolded protein response (UPR) element TGACGTGR (R is for A or G) found in animals and some plant UPR genes (Martínez and Chrispeels 2003; Oh et al., 2003). In the present study, the Hex/TGA box motifs were overrepresented in the DE cyclic genes in MO and Mature-EOD, whereas the canonical G box was enriched only in MO (Table I). This might imply that, during FL acclimation, light signals predominantly act in MO, while Hex/TGA-related signals impinge both in MO and at EOD. Circadian gating of light signal transduction offers another possible explanation for the overrepresentation of G box in the DE morning genes.

 The results in Fig. 9 and Table I also pointed to parallel downregulation of cell cycle-related evening genes having E2F motifs. The retinoblastoma/E2F pathway controls cell cycle and DNA replication in mammalian and plant cells (Stals and Inzé, 2001; Trimarchi and Lees, 2002). The activation of E2F/DP transcription factors, which control the entry to DNA replication phase, is regulated by growth and stress signals such as sugars and hormones (Stals and Inzé, 2001). Changes in these signals could alter the expression of E2F motif-containing cell cycle genes. Diel variations of starch and hormone levels (Bläsing et al., 2005; Nováková et al., 2005), supported by circadian regulation of transcription in these pathways (Harmer et al., 2000; Smith et al., 2004; Covington et al., 2008; Michael et al., 2008a), may underlie cyclic expression of cell cycle genes in leaves. Interestingly, Young leaves downregulated E2F motif-containing evening genes in MO (Fig. 9B, C; Table I) when hypocotyl growth exhibits a circadian peak in Arabidopsis (Nozue et al., 2007). In contrast, downregulation in Mature leaves was more pronounced at EOD when the expression of these genes reaches the maximum in light/dark conditions (Fig. 9B, C). Since cell proliferation ceases at an early stage of leaf development in

 Arabidopsis (Andriankaja et al., 2011), the repression of cell cycle genes found in Young and Mature leaves under the FL condition may reflect endoreduplication rather than cell division.

# **FL Induces SAR-like Gene Expression in Young Leaves**

 Alongside the time-of-day-dependent adjustments of gene expression, our results draw attention to the distinct gene expression responses in Young and Mature leaves especially at EOD (Fig. 4). Young leaves synthesize cell components as they grow whereas Mature leaves mostly do maintenance in non-stressful conditions. In addition, long-term acclimation involves biochemical and morphological alterations in Young leaves whereas Mature leaves must rely on the former. Despite the similar phenotypic alterations found for PSII (Fig. 2A-C) and metabolites (Fig. 3; Supplemental Figure 1), these leaves differ in acclimatory reprogramming of gene expression.

 Unexpectedly, we found FL-induced upregulation of SAR-like genes in Young leaves (Fig. 6; Supplemental Figure S4A, B) which also had higher SA contents than Mature leaves (Supplemental Figure S5A, B). While FL did not enhance SA accumulation, it tended to increase Pip (Supplemental Figure S5C) even though the increase was rather small compared to the levels seen after pathogen inoculation (Návarová et al., 2012). The specific patterns of SAR-like gene expression found in Young leaves under FL (Fig. 6) might be explained by concerted actions of Pip and SA (Bernsdorff et al., 2016). The SA-dependent *PR* gene induction is mediated by NPR1 (NONEXPRESSOR OF PR GENES 1), which moves from the cytoplasm to the nucleus in a redox-dependent manner (Mou et al., 2003). In the nucleus, NPR1 forms a complex with TGA box-binding factors (Johnson et al., 2003; Zhang et al., 2003). This pathway is constitutively active in the *cpr5* mutant, which accumulates large amounts of SA (Bowling et al., 1997). Whether the FL-induced SAR-like gene expression engages the NPR1-dependent pathway is not known.

 Marked SA accumulation has been reported in the *ceh1* (*constitutively expressing hydroperoxide lyase 1*) mutant, which exhibits upregulation of *ICS1* and other stress-responsive genes, e.g. marker genes of SA, JA and UPR (Xiao et al., 2012; Walley et al., 2015; Lemos et al., 2016). The mutation in *ceh1* causes amino acid substitution in the MEP pathway enzyme 1- hydorxy-2-*C*-methyl-2-(*E*)-butenyl-4-diphosphate synthase, resulting in accumulation of its substrate MEcPP. Increased accumulation of MEcPP has been detected in Arabidopsis leaves under HL or upon wounding, which was accompanied by transcriptional activation of stress- responsive genes (Xiao et al., 2012). Similarly, the reactive electrophile species β-cyclocitral was shown to upregulate *ICS1* to enhance SA accumulation (Lv et al., 2015). Unlike *ceh1* and β-cyclocitral treatment, however, our FL condition neither upregulated these genes nor increased

SA in leaves (Supplemental Figure S5A). Thus, the upregulation of SAR-like genes found in Young leaves (Fig. 6; Supplemental Figure S4), but not in Mature leaves, is distinct from the stress responses triggered by MEcPP and  $\beta$ -cyclocitral. The differences may be partly explained by the mild effects of our FL treatment which did not cause severe stress symptoms.

751

### 752 CONCLUSIONS

753 Acclimation to photo-oxidative FL conditions encompasses global reprogramming of gene 754 expression in Arabidopsis leaves, including genes for photoprotection, photosynthesis and 755 photorespiration as well as pigment, prenylquinone and vitamin metabolism. Since many genes undergo day/night cyclic expression in leaves, long-term large-scale adjustment of gene 756 757 expression proceeds in the temporal framework defined by diel and circadian programs. To identify acclimatory gene modules and understand their regulatory networks - also in 758 combination with different abiotic and biotic stress factors - remains a key research target to 759 760 develop strategies for improving plant performance in changing environments.

- 761
- 762

## 763 MATERIALS AND METHODS

## 764 Plant Materials and Growth Conditions

765 Seeds of Arabidopsis thaliana (L.) Heynh. Columbia-0 (Col-0) were sown on moist soil (type Pikier; 766 Balster Einheitserdewerk; Fröndenberg, Germany) and stratified at 5°C in the dark for 4 d before being 767 transferred to the climate chamber. The initial growth condition in the climate chamber was 12h/12h 768 light/dark cycle with 23°C/18°C air temperature at 60% relative air humidity. The intensity of photosynthetically active radiation provided by fluorescent tubes (Fluora L36 W/77; Osram, Munich, 769 Germany) was ~75  $\mu$ mol photons m<sup>-2</sup> s<sup>-1</sup> (CL). After two weeks, seedlings were transferred to pots (7 x 7 x 770 8 cm, one plant per pot) filled with soil (type ED 73; Balster Einheitserdewerk). After two or three more 771 772 weeks of cultivation in the CL condition, plants were divided into two groups. One group was kept in CL while the other group was transferred to a FL condition in which 20-s pulses of ~1000  $\mu$ mol photons m<sup>-2</sup> s<sup>-1</sup> 773 774 were applied by white LED (IP65, as-Schwabe, Eutingen, Germany) moving over the plants every 5 min 775 under the CL condition during the light period. The duration and frequency of FL was controlled by the computer program as described previously (Alter et al., 2012). Although the application of HL pulses 776 increased the average light intensity (~137 µmol photons  $m^{-2} s^{-1}$ ) under FL, acclimatory responses of LL-777 778 grown Arabidopsis plants are essentially the same regardless of whether HL pulses did or did not increase the average light intensity under FL (Alter et al., 2012), suggesting that plants do not respond to changes 779 780 in the average light intensity.

781

## 782 Chlorophyll Fluorescence Analysis

783 Chlorophyll fluorescence measurements were performed with Imaging-PAM MAXI (Heinz Walz, 784 Effeltrich, Germany) at the end of the dark period on day 3. An area of interest was selected inside Young 785 and Mature leaves of three replicate plants (per condition) by avoiding the leaf margin. After measuring 786 the maximal PSII efficiency ( $F_v/F_m$ ), actinic light was applied by blue LED of MAXI-PAM to obtain rapid 787 light response curves of ETR and NPQ. Light intensity was increased stepwise from 0 to ~1075 µmol photons m<sup>-2</sup> s<sup>-1</sup> with a dwell time of 20 s at each step. The Chl fluorescence parameters were calculated 788 789 as  $F_v/F_m = (F_m - F_0)/F_m$ , NPQ =  $(F_m - F_m)/F_m$  and ETR = PAR x 0.84 x 0.5 x  $(F_m - F)/F_m$  where  $F_m$  and  $F_0$ are the maximal and minimal fluorescence intensity of dark-adapted leaves and F'<sub>m</sub> and F the maximal 790 791 and actual fluorescence intensity of illuminated leaves.

792

### 793 Gas exchange measurements

794 CO<sub>2</sub> exchange rates were measured in mature leaves of five replicate plants (per condition) in the 795 morning of day 3 using a LI-6400XT with a red and blue LED light source (LI-COR, Lincoln, NE, USA). Measurements were performed in the climate chamber in which the CL and FL plants were growing (air 796 temperature 23°C; relative humidity 60%). The intensity of the LED was set to 800 µmol photons m<sup>-2</sup> s<sup>-1</sup>. 797 Leaves were allowed to reach steady state under this light intensity and 400 ppm CO<sub>2</sub>, which took 20-30 798 799 min. During this initial stabilization leaf temperature increased to 26-26.5°C but did not change further until 800 the end of the measurement. The CO<sub>2</sub> concentration inside the measuring cuvette was adjusted stepwise 801 with a dwell time of 4-5 min at each step (400, 250, 150, 100, 50, 400, 550, 700, 900, 1100, 1300 and 802 1500 ppm). The A/C<sub>i</sub> curves were analyzed according to Sharkey et al. (2007) to estimate the maximum 803 rates of linear electron transport to regenerate RuBP (J<sub>max</sub>) and of carboxylation by Rubisco (V<sub>cmax</sub>).

804

#### 805 Growth Analyses

Leaf growth analysis was started at the onset of the FL and CL treatment (day 0). Projected rosette leaf area (Fig. 1B) was determined for each of the 45 and 42 plants of the FL and CL treatments, respectively, by using the GROWSCREEN-FLUORO method (Jansen et al., 2009). Measurements were repeated daily at around ZT4 when leaves were in almost horizontal positions. The mean relative growth rate (RGR, % d<sup>-1</sup>) during the experiment was calculated by fitting growth curves to an exponential function and multiplying the growth factor by 100.

- The above-ground part of 17 and 20 plants (for the FL and CL conditions, respectively) was harvested at the end of the experiment (day 7) to measure fresh and dry weight. Fresh weight was measured using an analytical balance (XS205 DualRange, Mettler-Toledo, Giessen, Germany) immediately after excision. Dry weight was determined after drying at 70°C until a constant mass was reached. Leaf mass per area was then calculated for each plant based on the dry weight and the projected rosette leaf area measured on day 7.
- 818

## 819 Pigment Analysis

Leaf disks (56 mm<sup>2</sup> from Young and 100 mm<sup>2</sup> from Mature leaves) were taken from four replicate 820 821 plants (per time point and per condition) in MO and at EOD of day 3 under the FL and CL conditions, 822 immediately frozen in liquid N<sub>2</sub> and stored at -80°C until extraction. Photosynthetic pigments were 823 extracted by grinding frozen leaf disks in 1 mL acetone. The homogenate was centrifuged at 13000 rpm 824 for 5 min and the supernatant was filtered (0.45 µm True Syringe Filter, Alltech Associates) before an 825 aliquot (20 µL) was injected into a HPLC system. Chlorophylls and carotenoids were separated on an 826 Allsphere ODS-1 column (5 µm, Alltech Associates) by using the method described by Matsubara et al. 827 (2005). Photosynthetic pigments were identified based on retention times and absorption spectra recorded 828 by Waters 996 photodiode array detector (Waters Corporations). Peak areas were integrated at 440 nm 829 (Chl a and caroteonids) or 470 nm (Chl b) with Waters Empower software to determine chlorophyll 830 contents per unit leaf area and levels of individual carotenoids relative to the total Chl.

831

#### 832 Measurements of Sucrose and Starch

833 Young and Mature leaves were harvested from four replicate plants (per time point and per condition) 834 in MO and at EOD of day 3 under the FL and CL conditions. After measuring the fresh weight with an 835 analytical balance (XS205 DualRange, Mettler-Toledo), leaves were shock-frozen in liquid N<sub>2</sub> and stored 836 at -80°C until extraction. Leaves were homogenized by using TissueLyser II (QIAGEN). Soluble sugars 837 were extracted twice with 400 µL 80% ethanol in 2 mM Hepes followed by twice with 400 µL 50% ethanol 838 in 2 mM Hepes, incubating each time for 15 min at 80°C. Soluble sugar contents were determined by 839 coupled enzymatic assay (Jones et al., 1977). Glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase, Glc-6-phosphate 840 isomerase and hexokinase were purchased from Roche Diagnostics and invertase from Carl Roth 841 (Karlsruhe, Germany). Absorption changes at 340 nm, arising from reduction of NADP<sup>+</sup> to NADPH, were 842 measured with a Synergy 2 microplate reader (BioTek).

The pellets from sugar extraction were re-suspended in 500  $\mu$ L water before autoclaving at 120°C and 0.1 MPa for 90 min. Starch was digested enzymatically using  $\alpha$ -amylase and amyloglucosidase (Roche Diagnostics). An aliquot (100  $\mu$ L) was taken from each sample, mixed with 400  $\mu$ L 50 mM sodium acetate buffer (pH 4.9) containing the enzymes and incubated at 37°C for 16 h for starch digestion. Subsequently, soluble sugar (Glc) was measured as described above.

848

# 849 Determination of Amino Acids and Pip

850 Young and Mature leaves were harvested in MO and at EOD of day 3 under the FL and CL conditions, 851 frozen in liquid N<sub>2</sub> and stored at -80°C until extraction. Twelve leaves from six plants were pooled together 852 for each of the three or four biological replicates for analyses of amino acids and defense metabolites (Pip, 853 SA, SA glucosides and JA). Free amino acids were extracted from 60 mg of homogenized frozen leaf material in 500  $\mu$ L 25% acetonitrile in 0.01 M HCI. After adding 10  $\mu$ L of heptylamine (2 mg mL<sup>-1</sup>), the 854 855 samples were mixed thoroughly for 15 min at room temperature and centrifuged at 14000 rpm for 4 min. 856 The supernatant (100 µL) was taken and amino acids were extracted with EZ:faast free amino acid 857 analysis kit for GC-MS (Phenomenex, Aschaffenburg, Germany) as specified in the manufacturer's

 instructions. Subsequently, the samples were dissolved in 70 µL dichloromethane and 3 µL was injected into GC-MS (GC 7890A, Agilent Technologies). Amino acids were separated on a silica capillary column (ZB-AAA 10 m x 0.25 mm, Phenomenex). The GC-MS measurements and amino acid quantification were performed as described by Návarová et al. (2012). The method did not allow determination of Arg and Met.

#### **Analysis of SA, SA glucosides and JA**

 For the analysis of SA, SA glucosides and JA, 150 mg of the homogenized frozen leaf material was taken from the samples described for amino acid analysis. After adding 600 μL of pre-heated (70°C) extraction buffer (water:1-propanol:HCl in 1:2:0.005), a mixture of D4-SA and dihydro-JA (3.33 ng µL-1 each) was added to each sample as internal standards. The samples were then mixed with 2 mL dichloromethane, and organic supernatants were separated for the analysis of SA and JA, while pellets and inorganic supernatants were collected for extraction of SA glucosides. Water was removed from the organic supernatants by using Na2SO4. To remove solvent, vapor-phase extraction was conducted with Porapak-Q absorbent (VCT-1/4X3-POR-Q, Analytical Research Systems). Metabolites were eluted from the collector trap with dichloromethane, and the sample volume was reduced to 30 µL before storage at - 20ºC.

 For extraction of SA glucosides, 1 mL 0.1 M HCl was added to the pellets and inorganic supernatants before mixing with 30 µL of the internal standard and heating to 100ºC for 30 min. After cooling, dichloromethane (2 mL) was added and organic and inorganic phases were separated. The organic phase was dried three times with Na2SO<sup>4</sup> and solvent was evaporated under N<sup>2</sup> gas stream. The extracts were methylated with dichloromethane (300 µL) and methanol (60 µL). The vapor-phase extraction was performed as described above.

 The sample mixture (4 μL) was injected into GC-MS (GC 7890A; Agilent Technologies) and separated on a fused silica capillary column (ZB-5MS 30 m x 0.25 mm, Zebron, Phenomenex). The separation was performed as described by Mishina and Zeier (2006). The mass spectra were recorded with a combined 5975C MS detector (Agilent Technologies) in the electron ionization mode. Peak areas of ion chromatograms were integrated for typical ions of the metabolites by using Agilent software.

### **Metabolic Profiling**

 For each of the six replicate samples (per time point and per condition), Mature leaves (~500 mg) were harvested from six or seven plants in MO and at EOD of day 7 under the FL and CL conditions, pooled together and immediately frozen in liquid N2. The frozen leaf samples were sent to Metabolomic Discoveries (Potsdam, Germany) for non-targeted profiling of metabolites. The methods of sample extraction and derivatization, instrument settings and data analysis are as described by Lisec et al. (2006). The concentrations of metabolites were normalized to the internal standards and data were tested for normality (Shapiro-Wilk-Test) and variance homogeneity (F-Test) using the appropriate statistical tests (Students test, Welch test, and Mann-Whitney test). Relative changes in the FL samples compared with the corresponding CL samples were then calculated for each metabolite. The changes were considered  significant when *P* < 0.05. Statistical analyses were performed using the software JMP Genomics 5.1 (SAS Institute Inc., Cary, NC, USA).

## **RNA-Seq Analysis**

 Young and Mature leaves were collected from three replicate plants (per time point and per condition) in MO and at EOD of day 3 under the FL and CL conditions. Leaves were immediately frozen in liquid N<sup>2</sup> and stored at -80˚C until RNA extraction.
